Title: Improved Berry-Esseen bounds for multivariate nonlinear statistics in convex distance

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.10022v1

Published Time: Thu, 08 Oct 2026 01:20:42 GMT

Number of Pages: 90

Markdown Content:
# Improved Berry–Esseen bounds for multivariate nonlinear statistics in convex distance 

Zhi-Jun Cai 1 Jing-Cai Yang 2 Zhuo-Song Zhang 3

> 1

Department of Statistics and Data Science, The Chinese University of Hong Kong zhijuncai@cuhk.edu.hk 

> 2

Department of Statistics and Data Science, Southern University of Science and Technology yangjc@sustech.edu.cn 

> 3

Department of Statistics and Data Science and Shenzhen International Center for Mathematics, Southern University of Science and Technology zhangzs3@sustech.edu.cn 

Abstract 

In this paper, we establish two nonasymptotic Berry–Esseen bounds over convex sets for the Gaussian approximation of multivariate nonlinear statistics. The statistics of interest can be written as a sum of independent centered random vectors plus a remainder that may depend on all observations. The first bound retains the classical factor 𝑑 1/4 in the contribution of the independent sum, where 𝑑 is the dimension, while controlling the remainder through its size and its sensitivity to replacing a single observation. The second bound expresses the contribution of the independent sum in terms of fourth moments and can allow the dimension to grow faster with the sample size. For sums of independent random vectors, it removes the logarithmic factor from an existing fourth moment bound without imposing additional moment assumptions. As applications, we apply these results to Polyak–Ruppert averaging for nonsmooth stochastic approximation, temporal difference learning with linear function approximation, and multivariate 𝑈 -statistics. The resulting bounds provide explicit Gaussian approximation errors and sufficient conditions under which these errors converge to zero as the dimension grows with the sample size. 

MSC 2020 subject classifications: Primary 60F05, 62E17; secondary 62H10, 62L20. 

Keywords: Berry–Esseen bounds; Nonlinear statistics; Stein’s method; Stochastic approximation; Temporal Difference Learning. 

# 1 Introduction 

Gaussian approximation over convex sets is a classical problem in multivariate probability. When the dimension grows with the sample size, the main question is not only whether a Gaussian limit exists, but also how the approximation error depends on the dimension. Many statistics have a leading fluctuation given by a sum of independent random vectors, and for such sums sharp Gaussian approximation bounds are available. The difficulty is to pass from the leading sum to the full statistic without losing the dimension dependence of the independent case. For 𝑥 ∈ R𝑑 , write |𝑥 | for its Euclidean norm, and let A denote the collection of all measurable convex subsets of R𝑑 . For R𝑑 -valued random vectors 𝑈 and 𝑉 , define 

𝑑 𝑐 (𝑈, 𝑉 ) := sup 

> 𝐴 ∈ A

P(𝑈 ∈ 𝐴 ) − P(𝑉 ∈ 𝐴 ) .

Í𝑛 𝑖 =1 Var (𝑋 𝑖 ) = 𝐼 𝑑 . If 𝑍 ∼ N ( 0, 𝐼 𝑑 ), then Bentkus (2003, 2005) proved 

𝑑 𝑐 (𝑊, 𝑍 ) ≤ 𝐶𝑑 1/4

𝑑 1/4 is thus the standard dimension dependence for Gaussian approximation over all convex sets. For sums of independent random vectors, Fang and Koike (2024) obtained a bound in terms of fourth moments for a general positive definite covariance matrix. In particular, under the normalization Var (𝑊 ) = 𝐼 𝑑 ,their result gives 

𝑑 𝑐 (𝑊, 𝑍 ) ≤ 𝐶𝑑 1/4𝛿 √︁ | log 𝛿 |,

where 𝛿 := min {( Í𝑛 𝑖 =1 E|𝑋 𝑖 |4)1/2, 1/𝑒 }. This estimate can give a weaker restriction on the growth of 𝑑 . For example, suppose that 𝑋 𝑖 = 𝑌 𝑖 /√𝑛 , where the 𝑌 𝑖 ’s are centered, independent and identically distributed, and isotropic, with E|𝑌 𝑖 |3 ≍ 𝑑 3/2 and E|𝑌 𝑖 |4 ≍ 𝑑 2. The first bound is then of order 𝑑 7/4𝑛 −1/2, whereas the second is of order 𝑑 5/4𝑛 −1/2 up to a logarithmic factor. Thus, when the dimension grows, the relevant issue is the actual size of E|𝑋 𝑖 |3 and E|𝑋 𝑖 |4, rather than their finiteness alone. Related Gaussian approximation results in growing dimension have also been obtained for hyperrectangles and for Wasserstein distance; see, for example, Bonis (2020); Chernozhukov et al. (2017); Fang and Koike (2021); Lopes (2022); Zhai (2018). Many statistics arising in practice are nonlinear, and there is a substantial literature on error bounds for their normal approximation. This bound has the optimal order in the sample size in many applications. Its leading dimension factor is, however, 𝑑 1/2, even though the independent sum admits the 𝑑 1/4 factor above. 2The preceding results lead to two natural questions. Can the 𝑑 1/4 factor for the independent sum be retained after a general remainder is added? Can the estimate based on fourth moments also be extended to nonlinear statistics while removing the logarithmic factor from the contribution of the independent sum? We answer both questions. First, we prove a general Berry–Esseen bound for 𝑇 = 𝑊 + 𝐷 in which the contribution of 𝑊 retains the factor 𝑑 1/4. The remainder is controlled through suitable 𝐿 𝑝 norms of 𝐷 and the replacement differences 𝐷 − 𝐷 {𝑖 } , without any independence assumption between 𝑊 and 𝐷 . Second, we prove an alternative bound in terms of fourth moments for nonlinear statistics. For independent sums ( 𝐷 = 0), it improves the bound of Fang and Koike (2024, Theorem 2.1) by removing the logarithmic factor, while retaining the 𝑑 1/4 dependence and requiring no additional moment assumptions. The resulting bound can allow the dimension to grow faster in common settings. Both bounds cover nonidentically distributed summands and general positive definite covariance matrices. We apply the general results to Polyak–Ruppert averaging for nonsmooth stochastic approximation, temporal difference learning with linear function approximation, and multivariate 𝑈 -Statistics. In each case, we obtain an explicit bound in convex distance and a condition relating the problem dimension to the sample size. The proofs combine Stein’s method with recursive arguments. The proof of the second main result, stated in Theorem 2.4, additionally employs an exchangeable pair argument, together with a symmetry argument in the spirit of Fang and Koike (2024). We remove the logarithmic factor by treating small and large replacement increments separately at the natural smoothing scale and keeping this separation throughout the integration. Throughout the paper, for a matrix or a multilinear map 𝐴 , ∥ 𝐴 ∥op denotes its operator norm, while ∥ 𝐴 ∥F

denotes the Frobenius norm when 𝐴 is a matrix. For an R𝑑 -valued random vector 𝑋 and 𝑝 ≥ 1, we write 

∥ 𝑋 ∥ 𝑝 := (E|𝑋 | 𝑝 )1/ 𝑝 for its 𝐿 𝑝 norm. For 𝐴 ∈ A and 𝑡 ∈ R, let 𝐴 𝑡 := {𝑥 ∈ R𝑑 : dist (𝑥, 𝐴 ) ≤ 𝑡 } if 𝑡 > 0, and 

𝐴 𝑡 := {𝑥 ∈ 𝐴 : dist (𝑥, 𝐴 𝑐 ) > −𝑡 } if 𝑡 < 0, where dist (𝑥, 𝐴 ) := inf 𝑦 ∈ 𝐴 |𝑥 − 𝑦 |. For a nonempty closed convex set 𝑄 ⊂ R𝑑 , let 𝑃 𝑄 (𝑥 ) := argmin 𝑦 ∈𝑄 |𝑦 − 𝑥 | denote the Euclidean projection of 𝑥 onto 𝑄 .The rest of the paper is organized as follows. Section 2 states the two general Berry–Esseen bounds. Section 3 gives the applications to nonsmooth stochastic approximation, temporal difference learning, and multivariate 𝑈 -Statistics. Section 4 proves the general results, and Section 5 proves the application theorems. Additional technical details are deferred to Appendices A–C.

In the preceding results, the contribution arising from the linear part 𝑊 is controlled in terms of the third moments of the 𝑋 𝑖 ’s. In applications where the nonlinear remainder 𝐷 is of smaller order and does not dominate the overall approximation error, this contribution can be further sharpened under fourth-moment assumptions. Using an exchangeable pair construction, we obtain an alternative bound involving the fourth moments of the 𝑋 𝑖 ’s. As illustrated by the multivariate 𝑈 -statistic example, this alternative bound may lead to less restrictive conditions on the growth of the dimension 𝑑 relative to the sample size 𝑛 that still ensure that the Gaussian approximation error over convex sets tends to zero. 43 Applications 

This section presents three applications of our main theorem: Polyak–Ruppert averaging for nonsmooth stochastic approximation, temporal difference learning with linear function approximation, and multivariate 

𝑈 -statistics. These applications demonstrate how the general result yields explicit Berry–Esseen bounds for nonlinear statistics arising in stochastic algorithms and classical statistical problems. 

3.1 Polyak–Ruppert averaging for nonsmooth stochastic approximation 

Let 𝐹 : R𝑑 ⇒ R 𝑑 be a set-valued map. We consider the problem of finding a point 𝑥 ∗ satisfying 0 ∈ 𝐹 (𝑥 ∗). (3.1) Throughout this subsection, we fix one such solution 𝑥 ∗. To approximate 𝑥 ∗, we use the stochastic approximation recursion 

𝑥 𝑘 +1 = 𝑥 𝑘 − 𝛼 𝑘 +1𝐺 𝛼 𝑘 +1 (𝑥 𝑘 , 𝜁 𝑘 +1), 𝑘 ≥ 0, (3.2) where 𝐺 : R++ × R𝑑 × R𝑑 → R𝑑 is an update map, 𝛼 𝑘 > 0 are stepsizes, and 𝜁 𝑘 denotes stochastic noise. The associated Polyak–Ruppert average is ¯𝑥 𝑛 = 1

𝑛 

> 𝑛 −1

∑︁ 

> 𝑘 =0

𝑥 𝑘 . (3.3) Our goal is to establish a nonasymptotic Berry–Esseen bound over convex sets for the tangent-space component of the Polyak–Ruppert average in (3.3). 

3.1.1 Background 

Stochastic approximation is a recursive method for solving equations from noisy observations. To explain the basic idea, suppose that the target point 𝑥 ∗ satisfies ℎ(𝑥 ∗) = 0, where ℎ(𝑥 ) := E𝐻 (𝑥, 𝜁 ). If ℎ were available in closed form, one could apply a deterministic root-finding method using ℎ(𝑥 𝑘 ). In many applications, however, 

ℎ(𝑥 𝑘 ) cannot be evaluated directly, whereas 𝐻 (𝑥 𝑘 , 𝜁 𝑘 +1) can be computed from a new observation 𝜁 𝑘 +1 and serves as a noisy approximation to ℎ(𝑥 𝑘 ). Stochastic approximation therefore replaces ℎ(𝑥 𝑘 ) by 𝐻 (𝑥 𝑘 , 𝜁 𝑘 +1)

at each iteration. The recursion (3.2) is a general version of this idea, in which the update map may also depend on the stepsize. The method originates from Robbins and Monro (1951); early distributional theory was developed by Sacks (1958) and Fabian (1968). See also the review of Lai (2003). The ordinary sample mean is a simple example. To estimate 𝜇 := E𝜁 , take 𝐺 𝛼 (𝑥, 𝜁 ) = 𝑥 − 𝜁 . If 

𝛼 𝑘 +1 = 1/( 𝑘 + 1), then (3.2) gives 𝑥 𝑘 = 𝑘 −1 Í𝑘 𝑗 =1 𝜁 𝑗 for 𝑘 ≥ 1. Thus, the usual sample mean can itself be viewed as a stochastic approximation procedure. Another standard example is stochastic optimization. Suppose that the goal is to minimize the population loss 𝑓 (𝑥 ) := Eℓ(𝑥, 𝜁 ), where ℓ(𝑥, 𝜁 ) is the loss associated with one random observation 𝜁 . When 𝑓 is smooth, its gradient is given by ∇ 𝑓 (𝑥 ) = E∇𝑥 ℓ(𝑥, 𝜁 ) under standard regularity conditions. Since this expectation is generally unavailable, one replaces ∇ 𝑓 (𝑥 𝑘 ) by the stochastic gradient ∇𝑥 ℓ(𝑥 𝑘 , 𝜁 𝑘 +1). Accordingly, taking 𝐺 𝛼 (𝑥, 𝜁 ) = ∇𝑥 ℓ(𝑥, 𝜁 ) in (3.2) yields stochastic gradient descent. At each iteration, stochastic approximation updates the current estimate using only one new observation. It therefore does not need to process all available data at every step, which makes it useful when the data set is large or when observations arrive one at a time; see, for example, Nemirovski et al. (2009); Toulis and Airoldi (2017); Chen et al. (2020). Even when 𝑥 𝑘 converges to 𝑥 ∗, the last iterate may still be sensitive to recent stochastic errors and to the choice of stepsize. Polyak–Ruppert averaging reduces these effects by averaging the iterates. Suppose that ℎ

5is differentiable at 𝑥 ∗, and let 𝐽 := ∇ℎ(𝑥 ∗) and 𝑆 := Var {𝐻 (𝑥 ∗, 𝜁 )} . Under standard stability and moment conditions, the classical averaging theory gives 

√𝑛 ( ¯𝑥 𝑛 − 𝑥 ∗) =⇒ N  0, 𝐽 −1𝑆𝐽 −⊤ .

See Polyak and Juditsky (1992); Kushner and Yang (1993); Ruppert (1988) for the classical theory and Toulis and Airoldi (2017); Chen et al. (2020); Duchi and Ruan (2021) for subsequent developments in statistical inference and asymptotic optimality. The smooth equation ℎ(𝑥 ∗) = 0 does not cover many nonsmooth optimization and estimation problems. As a simple example, consider the ℓ1-regularized problem of minimizing 𝑓 (𝑥 ) + 𝜆 |𝑥 |1, where 𝑓 (𝑥 ) := Eℓ(𝑥, 𝜁 )

is smooth, 𝜆 > 0, and |𝑥 |1 := Í𝑑 𝑗 =1 |𝑥 𝑗 | denotes the ℓ1-norm of 𝑥 . Its first-order optimality condition is 0 ∈ ∇ 𝑓 (𝑥 ∗) + 𝜆𝜕 | · | 1 (𝑥 ∗). Thus, the problem takes the form (3.1) with 𝐹 (𝑥 ) := ∇ 𝑓 (𝑥 ) + 𝜆𝜕 | · | 1 (𝑥 ). The map 𝐹 is set-valued because the subdifferential of the absolute value at zero is the interval [− 1, 1]. A natural stochastic algorithm for this problem is the proximal stochastic gradient update 𝑥 𝑘 +1 = prox 𝛼 𝑘 +1𝜆 | · | 1 {𝑥 𝑘 −

𝛼 𝑘 +1∇𝑥 ℓ(𝑥 𝑘 , 𝜁 𝑘 +1)} ; see, for example, Duchi and Singer (2009). This is a special case of (3.2), with 

𝐺 𝛼 (𝑥, 𝜁 ) := 𝛼 −1{𝑥 − prox 𝛼𝜆 | · | 1 (𝑥 − 𝛼 ∇𝑥 ℓ(𝑥, 𝜁 ))} .

Although 𝐹 may be nonsmooth in the ambient space, many problems have a locally smooth structure once the active pattern of the solution is fixed. In the ℓ1-regularized example, this pattern is determined by the zero coordinates of 𝑥 ∗ and the signs of its nonzero coordinates. More generally, under suitable regularity conditions, the active structure can be represented by a smooth manifold M containing 𝑥 ∗; see Wright (1993); Lewis (2002); Lewis and Zhang (2013). In the framework of Davis et al. (2024), the tangential map 

𝐹 M (𝑦 ) := 𝑃 𝑇 M ( 𝑦 ) 𝐹 (𝑦 ) is locally single-valued and smooth, while a separate proximal aiming condition controls the attraction of the stochastic iterates toward M.Using this local smooth structure, Davis et al. (2024) proved asymptotic normality of the Polyak–Ruppert average and local asymptotic optimality of its limiting covariance. Jiang et al. (2025) subsequently studied online estimation of the limiting covariance and established nonasymptotic bounds for the covariance-estimation error. These results do not, however, provide a finite-sample bound for the Gaussian approximation error of the averaged iterates in convex distance. For related finite-sample Gaussian approximation results, see Shao and Zhang (2022); Sheshukova et al. (2026) for averaged stochastic gradient descent with smooth objectives and Samsonov et al. (2024) for linear stochastic approximation. 

3.1.2 Smooth manifolds 

For the reader’s convenience, we first recall a few definitions from smooth manifolds. Throughout this subsection, all manifolds are assumed to be embedded in R𝑑 , and their tangent spaces are regarded as linear subspaces of R𝑑 . For 𝑝 ≥ 1, a set M ⊂ R𝑑 is called an 𝑚 -dimensional 𝐶 𝑝 embedded manifold if, for every 

𝑥 ∈ M , there exist an open neighborhood U ⊂ R𝑑 of 𝑥 and a 𝐶 𝑝 map 𝑓 : U → R𝑑 −𝑚 such that 

M ∩ U = 𝑓 −1 (0), rank ∇ 𝑓 (𝑥 ) = 𝑑 − 𝑚, 

where ∇ 𝑓 (𝑥 ) : R𝑑 → R𝑑 −𝑚 denotes the differential of 𝑓 at 𝑥 . The tangent and normal spaces to M at 𝑥 are defined, respectively, by 

𝑇 M (𝑥 ) = ker ∇ 𝑓 (𝑥 ), 𝑁 M (𝑥 ) = 𝑇 M (𝑥 )⊥.

For a 𝐶 𝑝 -smooth manifold M with 𝑝 ≥ 2, the projection 𝑃 M is 𝐶 𝑝 −1-smooth on a sufficiently small neighborhood of each point 𝑥 ∈ M ; see Miller and Malick (2005). Moreover, for every 𝑥 ∈ M ,

∇𝑃 M (𝑥 ) = 𝑃 𝑇 M ( 𝑥 ) ;6see Lewis and Malick (2008, Lemma 2.1). A map ℎ : M → R𝑟 is said to be 𝐶 𝑝 near 𝑥 ∈ M if there exist an open neighborhood U of 𝑥 in R𝑑 and a map bℎ ∈ 𝐶 𝑝 (U ; R𝑟 ) such that bℎ = ℎ on U ∩ M . For 𝑝 ≥ 1, its tangential Jacobian at 𝑥 is the linear map ∇M ℎ(𝑥 ) : 𝑇 M (𝑥 ) → R𝑟 defined by 

∇M ℎ(𝑥 )𝑣 := ∇bℎ(𝑥 )𝑣, 𝑣 ∈ 𝑇 M (𝑥 ).

Throughout the remainder of this subsection, let M be an embedded 𝑚 -dimensional 𝐶 4 submanifold of 

R𝑑 containing 𝑥 ∗. Let 𝑈 ∈ R𝑑 ×𝑚 be a matrix whose columns form an orthonormal basis of 𝑇 M (𝑥 ∗).Choose 𝛿 1 > 0 sufficiently small so that the nearest-point projection 𝑃 M is well-defined and 𝐶 3 on

3.1.3 Assumptions and main result 

To state our main result, we first introduce several assumptions. Classical results for stochastic approximation often assume that 𝐹 is smooth in a neighborhood of 𝑥 ∗ in the whole space R𝑑 . For the nonsmooth setting considered here, we follow the local manifold framework used by Davis et al. (2024) in their study of asymptotic normality. In particular, Assumption 3.1 does not require 𝐹 to be smooth in the whole space. It only requires the tangent component 𝐹 M to be smooth near 𝑥 ∗ along a smooth manifold M. The assumption also requires 𝐹 M to be locally Lipschitz and its Riemannian Jacobian at 𝑥 ∗ to be positive and bounded on the tangent space 𝑇 M (𝑥 ∗).

Assumption 3.1. Suppose that there exists a 𝐶 4 manifold M ⊂ R𝑑 containing 𝑥 ∗ such that 

𝐹 M (𝑥 ) := 𝑃 𝑇 M ( 𝑥 ) 𝐹 (𝑥 ), 𝑥 ∈ M ,

is single valued and 𝐶 2 on a neighborhood of 𝑥 ∗ in M. Furthermore, 𝐹 M is Lipschitz with constant 𝐿 M > 0there. The local extension b𝐹 M := 𝐹 M ◦ 𝑃 M , defined on a sufficiently small neighborhood of 𝑥 ∗ in R𝑑 , has second derivative bounded in operator norm by a constant 𝑀 2 > 0 at every 𝑦 ∈ M sufficiently close to 𝑥 ∗.Moreover, there are constants 0 < 𝜆 1 ≤ 𝜆 2 such that 

𝜆 1 |𝑣 |2 ≤ ∇M 𝐹 M (𝑥 ∗)𝑣, 𝑣 , ∥∇ M 𝐹 M (𝑥 ∗)∥ op ≤ 𝜆 2 (3.5) for every 𝑣 ∈ 𝑇 M (𝑥 ∗).Assumption 3.1 describes the smooth and stable dynamics intrinsic to M. We next impose a local compatibility condition between these dynamics and the stochastic update map 𝐺 𝛼 . Conditions (i) and (ii) below control the error in approximating the tangential component of 𝐺 𝛼 (𝑥, 𝜁 ) by 𝐹 M (𝑃 M (𝑥 ))+ 𝑃 𝑇 M ( 𝑃 M ( 𝑥 ) ) 𝜁 ,together with the local variation of this error with respect to (𝑥, 𝜁 ). Condition (iii) imposes a quantitative proximal aiming condition in the normal direction, which provides the mechanism for controlling the distance of the iterates from M. These requirements are made precise in the following assumption. 

Assumption 3.2. There are constants 𝑐 1, 𝑐 2, 𝑐 3, 𝑐 4 > 0, a neighborhood U of 𝑥 ∗, and U𝐹 := U ∩ dom 𝐹 

such that the following statements hold for all 𝑥, 𝑥 ′ ∈ U 𝐹 , all 𝜁, 𝜁 ′ ∈ R𝑑 , and all 𝛼 > 0. Define

(i) The residual R𝛼 satisfies 

|R 𝛼 (𝑥, 𝜁 )| ≤ 𝑐 1 (1 + | 𝑥 − 𝑥 ∗ |2 + | 𝜁 |2){ dist (𝑥, M) + 𝛼 }.

7(ii) The difference between two residuals satisfies 

|R 𝛼 (𝑥, 𝜁 ) − R 𝛼 (𝑥 ′, 𝜁 ′)| ≤ 𝑐 2 (1 + | 𝜁 |2 + | 𝜁 ′ |2){ 𝛼 + | 𝑥 − 𝑥 ∗ | + | 𝑥 ′ − 𝑥 ∗ |}{| 𝑥 − 𝑥 ′ | + | 𝜁 − 𝜁 ′ |} .

To control the iterates globally, we adopt the following stability assumption from Jiang et al. (2025). The first inequality ensures that the update points toward 𝑥 ∗, while the second controls the growth of the update. These conditions will be used to establish 𝐿 𝑝 -bounds for the iterates. 

Assumption 3.3. There are constants 𝑐 5, 𝑐 6, 𝑐 7 > 0 such that, for every 𝑥 ∈ R𝑑 , 𝜁 ∈ R𝑑 , and 𝛼 > 0,

|𝐺 𝛼 (𝑥, 𝜁 )| 2 ≤ 𝑐 7{1 + | 𝑥 − 𝑥 ∗ |2 + | 𝜁 |2}.

The next assumption specifies conditions on the iterates and the noise sequence. Condition (i) requires the iterates to remain in dom 𝐹 and bounds the twelfth moment of the initial error. Condition (ii) decomposes the noise 𝜁 𝑘 into an independent innovation 𝜉 𝑘 and a state-dependent martingale difference component 𝜂 𝑘 ,and specifies their moment, covariance, and regularity properties. A similar assumption appears in Shao and Zhang (2022).

Remark 3.7. The factor 𝑈 ⊤ in Theorem 3.6 is essential. Davis et al. (2024) showed that the limiting fluctuations of √𝑛 ( ¯𝑥 𝑛 − 𝑥 ∗) are supported on 𝑇 M (𝑥 ∗). Accordingly, the natural Gaussian approximation in the ambient space R𝑑 is 𝑈 Σ1/2

Thus, the natural ambient-space Gaussian approximation does not satisfy the nondegeneracy condition required by Corollary 2.3. There is also a more basic obstruction coming from the convex-set distance. Set 𝑇 ∗ := 𝑇 M (𝑥 ∗). Since 𝑇 ∗

is a convex subset of R𝑑 and 𝑈 Σ1/2

Since the collection of convex sets is invariant under orthogonal transformations, the corresponding convex-set distance is unchanged. When 𝑚 = 𝑑 , the matrix 𝑈 is square and orthogonal, so the tangent-coordinate and ambient-space formulations are equivalent.

3.3 Multivariate 𝑈 -statistics 

A 𝑈 -statistic is obtained by averaging a symmetric kernel over all subsamples of a fixed size. Introduced by Hoeffding (1948), this class includes many familiar statistics, such as the sample mean, the sample variance, and rank-based statistics. For a nondegenerate scalar 𝑈 -statistic, the Hoeffding decomposition reduces the leading term to a sum of independent random variables, and the classical Berry–Esseen rate of order 𝑛 −1/2 is available under a third-moment condition; see, for example, Callaert and Janssen (1978); Chen and Shao (2007). Multivariate Berry–Esseen and Edgeworth approximations were studied by Helmers and Janssen (1982) and Götze (1987). More recently, Chen (2018) established high-dimensional Gaussian and bootstrap approximations over hyperrectangles. Here we consider a vector of 𝑈 -statistics whose coordinates may have different orders and derive normal-approximation bounds over the class of all convex sets. 12 Let 𝑋 1, . . . , 𝑋 𝑛 be independent and identically distributed random variables taking values in a measurable space X. # 4 Proof of main results 

In this section, we prove the main results. We begin by establishing several preliminary lemmas and propositions needed in the subsequent proofs. The remaining two subsections are devoted, respectively, to the proofs of Theorem 2.1 and Corollary 2.3, and to those of Theorem 2.4 and Corollary 2.6. Throughout this section, 𝐶 denotes a positive absolute constant whose value may vary from line to line.

Proof of Corollary 2.3. For any 𝐴 ∈ A , set 𝐵 = Σ −1/2 𝐴 := {Σ−1/2𝑥 : 𝑥 ∈ 𝐴 }. Since an invertible linear transformation preserves convexity, 𝐵 ∈ A . Moreover, 

P(𝑇 ∈ 𝐴 ) − P(Σ1/2 𝑍 ∈ 𝐴 ) = P(Σ−1/2𝑇 ∈ 𝐵 ) − P(𝑍 ∈ 𝐵 ).

Apply Theorem 2.1 to Σ−1/2𝑇 with 𝑟 = 2, 𝑞 = max {6, 12 log 𝑑 }, and 𝑠 = 2𝑞 /( 𝑞 − 2) ≤ 3. Since 

∥Σ−1/2 ∥op ≤ 𝜎 −1/2 and 𝑞𝑑 1/( 2𝑞 ) ≤ 𝐶 (1 + log 𝑑 ), the desired bound follows. 

□

𝐾 𝑛 ≤ 𝐶 + 𝐶𝐾 1/𝑞 𝑛 + 𝐶𝐾 1/2 

> 𝑛

+ 12 𝐾 𝑛 .

Since 𝑞 ≥ 4, we have 𝐾 1/𝑞 𝑛 ≤ 1 + 𝐾 1/2 

> 𝑛

. Young’s inequality therefore gives 𝐶𝐾 1/𝑞 𝑛 + 𝐶𝐾 1/2 

> 𝑛

≤ 𝐾 𝑛 /4 + 𝐶. 

Consequently, we have 

𝐾 𝑛 ≤ 𝐶 + 34 𝐾 𝑛 .

Since 𝐾 𝑛 < ∞, rearranging this inequality yields 𝐾 𝑛 ≤ 𝐶 ‡ uniformly in 𝑞 ≥ 4, proving the auxiliary bound. Taking 𝑞 = max {4, (log 𝑑 )/ 2} and using 𝑞𝑑 1/( 2𝑞 ) ≤ 𝐶 (1 + log 𝑑 ) completes the proof. 

□

Proof of Corollary 2.6. Observe that 

P(𝑇 ∈ 𝐴 ) − P(Σ1/2 𝑍 ∈ 𝐴 ) = P(Σ−1/2𝑇 ∈ Σ−1/2 𝐴 ) − P(𝑍 ∈ Σ−1/2 𝐴 ).

Since Σ−1/2 𝐴 is convex whenever 𝐴 is convex, the conclusion follows by applying Theorem 2.4. □

# 5 Proofs of other results 

In this section, we prove Theorems 3.6, 3.9, and 3.12, corresponding respectively to the applications to nonsmooth stochastic approximation, temporal-difference learning with linear function approximation, and multivariate 𝑈 -statistics. ∥𝐺 (𝑋 1)∥ 4. This proves (3.21). □

# Appendix B. Additional details on nonsmooth stochastic approximation 

In this section, we provide additional details for the application to nonsmooth stochastic approximation. We first present a coupled nonsmooth example and then prove Lemmas 5.1 and 5.2. 

B.1 A coupled nonsmooth example 

We now give a simple example showing that Assumptions 3.1–3.5 can hold simultaneously. The example is a stochastic forward–backward method with a fusion penalty. Consider the problem of finding 𝑥 ∗ ∈ R𝑑 such that 0 ∈ 𝐴 (𝑥 ∗) + 𝜕 𝑓 (𝑥 ∗),

where 𝐴 : R𝑑 → R𝑑 is a single valued smooth operator and 𝑓 : R𝑑 → (−∞ , +∞] is a proper lower semicontinuous convex function. For 𝛼 > 0, the proximal map of 𝑓 is defined by prox 𝛼 𝑓 (𝑧 ) := arg min

The stochastic forward–backward method uses a noisy evaluation 𝐴 (𝑥 𝑘 ) + 𝜁 𝑘 +1 of the smooth operator and updates the iterate by

(B.1) The deterministic forward–backward splitting framework can be traced back at least to Lions and Mercier (1979); see Combettes and Wajs (2005) for an influential treatment of proximal forward–backward splitting in convex optimization. Stochastic forward–backward methods for monotone inclusion problems were studied in Rosasco et al. (2016); Combettes and Pesquet (2016). For 𝑚 ≥ 1, let 𝑑 = 2𝑚 , and write 𝑥 = (𝑢, 𝑣 ) ∈ R𝑚 × R𝑚 . M := {( 𝑧, 𝑧 ) : 𝑧 ∈ R𝑚 }.

B.3 Proof of Lemma 5.2 

In this subsection, we prove Lemma 5.2. The proof is organized as follows. We first establish several auxiliary lemmas that will be used repeatedly. We then derive moment and replacement bounds for the main components of 𝐷 𝑛 , and finally combine these componentwise estimates to prove Lemma 5.2.

B.3.2 Completion of the proof 

We now complete the proof of Lemma 5.2. For clarity, the argument is divided into two steps. We first prove Lemmas B.7–B.9, which provide separate bounds for the terms in the decomposition of 𝐷 𝑛 and their corresponding replacement differences. We then combine these bounds to obtain the conclusion of Lemma 5.2.

# Appendix C. Further details for additional applications 

This section provides further details for the applications to temporal-difference learning with linear function approximation and multivariate 𝑈 -statistics. For temporal-difference learning, we prove Lemma 5.3, show that the two additional terms arising in the proof of Theorem 3.9 can be absorbed into the right-hand side of (3.14), and verify the dimension-only rates. For multivariate 𝑈 -statistics, we prove Lemma 5.4. 78 C.1 Proof of Lemma 5.3
